B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

接受 DAPT 或 OAC 的登革热患者面临更频繁的出血 - Medscape

Dengue Patients on DAPT or OAC Face More Frequent Bleeding - Medscape
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 29 日发表在《国际传染病杂志》上的一项研究发现,与未接受抗血栓治疗 (ATT) 的患者相比,接受双重抗血小板治疗 (DAPT) 或口服抗凝治疗 (OAC) 的登革热患者在 诊断 治疗的 7 和 30 天内经历更频繁的大出血事件。这项研究由台湾台南成功大学医院的 Po-Tseng Lee 领导,分析了 2015 年至 2022 年间诊断出的登革热患者的数据。研究显示,7 天内,无 ATT 组的大出血率为 0.34%,单一抗血小板治疗 (SAPT) 组为 0.61%,DAPT 组为 4.55%,OAC 组为 6.67%。30 天时,这些比率分别增加至 1.15%、1.84%、9.09% 和 13.33%。还注意到主要不良心血管事件,无 ATT 组有 1 名患者,DAPT 组有 3 名患者。该研究强调,接受 ATT 治疗的患者可能是弱势群体,但也承认其局限性,包括事件数据稀疏以及由于缺乏系统的 治疗 信息而可能导致暴露错误分类。研究结果表明,在接受抗血栓治疗时需要谨慎管理登革热患者。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2026年8月29日在线发表,事件日期为登革热诊断后7至30天内。
地点与对象
该研究在台湾台南国立成功大学医院进行,涉及 2015 年至 2022 年间感染登革热的患者。
事件
一项研究发现,与未接受抗血栓治疗 (ATT) 的患者相比,接受双重抗血小板治疗 (DAPT) 或口服抗凝治疗 (OAC) 的登革热患者在 诊断 治疗 7 和 30 天内出现大出血的几率更高。这些发现是由李宝增领导的研究人员报告的。
规模、影响与风险
诊断治疗7天内,无ATT组大出血率为0.34%,SAPT组为0.61%,DAPT组为4.55%,OAC组为6.67%。30天内,利率分别为1.15%、1.84%、9.09%和13.33%。还观察到主要不良心血管事件,无 ATT 组有 1 名患者,DAPT 组有 3 名患者。研究指出,这些发现表明接受 ATT 治疗的患者属于临床上的弱势群体。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是一项单中心观察性研究,采用非随机 治疗 分配,导致 ATT 组之间的风险基线存在差异。作者承认存在局限性,包括稀疏的大出血和心血管事件,特别是在 DAPT 和 OAC 组中,这影响了风险估计的准确性和可靠性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue Patients on DAPT or OAC Face More Frequent Bleeding - Medscape

News Brief
A study published on August 29, 2026, in the International Journal of Infectious Diseases found that dengue patients on dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) or oral anticoagulant therapy (OAC) experienced more frequent major bleeding events within 7 and 30 days of diagnosis compared to those not on antithrombotic therapy (ATT). The research, led by Po-Tseng Lee at National Cheng Kung University Hospital in Tainan, Taiwan, analyzed data from patients diagnosed with dengue between 2015 and 2022. The study revealed that within 7 days, major bleeding rates were 0.34% in the no ATT group, 0.61% in the single antiplatelet therapy (SAPT) group, 4.55% in the DAPT group, and 6.67% in the OAC group. At 30 days, these rates increased to 1.15%, 1.84%, 9.09%, and 13.33%, respectively. Major adverse cardiovascular events were also noted, with 1 patient in the no ATT group and 3 in the DAPT group. The study highlighted that patients on ATT may be a vulnerable group, but it acknowledged limitations, including sparse event data and potential exposure misclassification due to lack of systematic treatment information. The findings suggest the need for caution in managing dengue patients on antithrombotic therapies.
Original source text (English)
Medscape AI News & Perspective Tools & Reference CME/CE More NEW! Ask complex medical questions and find the latest clinical guidance for drugs & conditions with Medscape AI× EN RegisterLog In AI Mode Medscape Medical NewsMedBrief Medbrief Dengue Patients on Dual Antiplatelet Therapy or Oral Anticoagulants Face More Frequent Bleeding September 01, 2026Int J Infect Dis. 2026-08-29;:109087.doi:10.1016/j.ijid.2026.109087 0 Add to Email Alerts TOPLINE Patients with acute dengue infection who were on dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) or oral anticoagulant therapy (OAC) had higher rates of major bleeding within 7 days of diagnosis than those not receiving antithrombotic therapy (ATT), whereas those on single antiplatelet therapy (SAPT) showed major bleeding rates similar to those not receiving ATT at both 7 and 30 days after diagnosis. METHODOLOGY Researchers conducted a single-center, observational study to evaluate real-world bleeding and cardiovascular outcomes in patients with acute dengue infection receiving pre-existing ATT. The study comprised a retrospective cardiovascular cohort that used data from a hospital database to identify patients who developed dengue infection between 2015 and 2022 and an exploratory platelet-monitoring cohort that tracked platelet count and platelet function during dengue illness. Patients in the retrospective cohort were classified into four groups based on pre-existing ATT at dengue diagnosis: no ATT (n = 1493; mean age, 63.2 years; 50.5% men), SAPT (n = 163; mean age, 71.7 years; 67.5% men), DAPT (n = 22; mean age, 70.1 years; 63.6% men), and OAC (n = 15; mean age, 70.3 years; 73.3% men). The exploratory cohort included patients not receiving antiplatelet therapy (n = 6) and those receiving aspirin (n = 2) who underwent serial platelet count and platelet function assessments during dengue illness to evaluate platelet recovery patterns. Outcomes were major bleeding events and major adverse cardiovascular events assessed within 7 and 30 days after dengue diagnosis. TAKEAWAY Patients receiving pre-existing ATT were significantly older and experienced more frequent severe dengue manifestations, including severe plasma leakage and severe organ involvement, than those not receiving ATT (P < .001). Within 7 days of dengue diagnosis, major bleeding rates were 0.34% in the no ATT group, 0.61% in the SAPT group, 4.55% in the DAPT group, and 6.67% in the OAC group (P = .011); major adverse cardiovascular events occurred in 1 patient in the no ATT group and 3 patients in the DAPT group, with no events in the SAPT or OAC groups. Within 30 days of dengue diagnosis, major bleeding rates were 1.15% in the no ATT group, 1.84% in the SAPT group, 9.09% in the DAPT group, and 13.33% in the OAC group (P = .002); major adverse cardiovascular events occurred in 7 patients in the no ATT group, 2 patients in the SAPT group, and 3 patients in the DAPT group, with no events in the OAC group. In the exploratory cohort, recovery of platelet counts and platelet function was slower among patients receiving aspirin than among those not receiving antiplatelet therapy during dengue illness, with median recovery times of 7.5 vs 4.0 days and 9.5 vs 4.0 days, respectively (P = .04 for both). IN PRACTICE "These findings suggest that patients receiving pre-existing ATT, including those receiving SAPT, appeared to represent a clinically vulnerable group in this cohort," the authors wrote. SOURCE The study was led by Po-Tseng Lee, National Cheng Kung University Hospital, Tainan, Taiwan. It was published online on August 29 in International Journal of Infectious Diseases. LIMITATIONS The single-center observational design and nonrandomized treatment allocation resulted in baseline differences in risk across ATT groups. Major bleeding and cardiovascular events were sparse, particularly in the DAPT and OAC groups, which limited the precision and reliability of the estimated risks. Classification of ATT at dengue diagnosis may have led to exposure misclassification because information on treatment adherence, last dose, treatment interruption, resumption timing, platelet thresholds, and anticoagulant intensity was not systematically available. DISCLOSURES The study was supported by a research grant from the National Health Research Institutes and the Ministry of Science and Technology, Taiwan, Republic of China. The authors declared having no known competing financial interests or personal relationships that could have influenced the reported work. 佛罗里达州登革热激增,一周内报告新增 18 例 - The Florida Times-Union

Dengue surges in Florida with 18 new cases reported in one week - The Florida Times-Union
新闻简报据佛罗里达州卫生部称,佛罗里达州 登革病毒 病例激增,截至 2026 年 8 月 31 日,单周报告新增本地感染病例 18 例。这使得 2026 年全州病例总数达到 41 例,其中希尔斯伯勒县占 30 例。佛罗里达州的八个县,包括希尔斯伯勒县、迈阿密戴德县和皮内拉斯县,均处于蚊媒疾病咨询或警报状态,表明疾病活动加剧或 媒介 人口增加。佛罗里达州卫生部 (FDOH) 建议公众采取预防措施,例如使用避蚊胺驱虫剂并实施“排水和覆盖”策略,以减少家中的蚊子暴露。虽然 监测 已得到确认,但确切的利差和风险水平尚未明确详细说明,FDOH 继续通过每周 监测 报告监控情况。登革热症状包括突然发烧、剧烈头痛、肌肉和关节疼痛以及出血,可持续四到七天。该病毒主要通过蚊子传播,目前 暴发 尚无死亡报告。FDOH 还强调了将登革热与西尼罗河病毒和寨卡病毒等其他蚊媒疾病区分开来的重要性,这些疾病受到监测,但不是本报告的主要重点。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至 2026 年 8 月 31 日,登革热病例激增,一周内新增 18 例(N2、N6、N7)。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在佛罗里达州,特别是希尔斯伯勒县,该县占 2026 年全州报告的 41 例本地感染登革热病例中的 30 例(N3、N17)。
事件
佛罗里达州 登革病毒 病例激增,佛罗里达州卫生部 (N6、N7) 一周内报告新增 18 例本地感染病例。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年8月31日,佛罗里达州已有41例本地感染登革热病例,单周新增病例18例。八个县已收到蚊媒疾病建议或警报,包括希尔斯伯勒、迈阿密戴德县和皮内拉斯(N3、N4、N17)。
应对、状态与不确定性
佛罗里达州卫生部 (FDOH) 建议使用基于避蚊胺的驱虫剂和“排水和覆盖”策略来减少蚊子暴露。他们还通过每周报告(N5、N12、N13、N16)监测蚊媒疾病。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue surges in Florida with 18 new cases reported in one week - The Florida Times-Union

News Brief
Dengue virus has seen a surge in Florida, with 18 new locally acquired cases reported in a single week as of August 31, 2026, according to the Florida Department of Health. This brings the statewide total for 2026 to 41 cases, with Hillsborough County accounting for 30 of them. Eight Florida counties, including Hillsborough, Miami-Dade, and Pinellas, are under mosquito-borne illness advisories or alerts, indicating heightened disease activity or increased vector populations. The Florida Department of Health (FDOH) advises the public to take precautions such as using DEET-based repellents and implementing a "drain and cover" strategy to reduce mosquito exposure at home. While the outbreak is confirmed, the exact spread and risk levels are not explicitly detailed, and the FDOH continues to monitor the situation through weekly surveillance reports. Dengue symptoms include sudden fever, severe headache, muscle and joint pain, and bleeding, which can last four to seven days. The virus is primarily spread by mosquitoes, and there are no reported fatalities in the current outbreak. The FDOH also highlights the importance of distinguishing dengue from other mosquito-borne illnesses like West Nile virus and Zika virus, which are monitored but not the primary focus of this report.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Entertainment Opinion Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals STATE Dengue surges in Florida with 18 new cases reported in one week Cheryl McCloud USA TODAY NETWORK - Florida Updated Aug. 31, 2026, 12:17 p.m. ET Florida has reported 41 locally acquired dengue cases in 2026, with 18 new cases in a single week; Hillsborough County accounts for 30. Eight Florida counties are under mosquito-borne illness advisories or alerts, including Hillsborough, Miami-Dade, and Pinellas. FDOH recommends DEET-based repellents and a "drain and cover" strategy to reduce mosquito exposure at home. Cases of dengue continue to increase across Florida, with 18 new locally acquired cases reported in one week by the Florida Department of Health. That brings the statewide total of dengue cases to 41 for the year as of Aug. 22. ➤ Meet the mosquito, the world's deadliest animal The mosquito-borne illness is one of several monitored by the agency. Eight counties are under either a mosquito-borne illness advisory or mosquito-borne illness alert. Shop Amazon for mosquito repellents Here's what you should know about mosquito-borne illnesses in Florida, symptoms to watch for and how best to prevent mosquito bites. How many types of mosquito-borne illnesses are there in Florida? FDOH compiles weekly data on the following mosquito-borne viruses in a report called "Florida Arbovirus Surveillance": West Nile virus (WNV) Eastern equine encephalitis virus (EEEV) St. Louis encephalitis virus (SLEV) Dengue virus (DENV) Chikungunya virus (CHIKV) Zika virus (ZIKV) California encephalitis group viruses (CEV) Malaria Mosquito-borne illness alerts, advisories issued for these Florida counties A mosquito-borne illness advisory has been issued for the following counties: Alachua Leon Palm Beach Pasco Walton A mosquito-borne illness alert has been issued for the following counties: Hillsborough Miami-Dade Pinellas "A mosquito-borne illness advisory means the public should take precautions to reduce mosquito bites due to factors indicating local disease such as positive sentinels, very few presumptive or positive human cases, or other justifications," according to Sarasota County. "A mosquito-borne illness alert means that there has been heightened disease activity, a significant increase in vector (disease capable) mosquito populations, or an increase in locally-transmitted human cases. "Residents should continue to take precautions, but exercise additional caution in preventing mosquito bites." Is dengue spreading in Florida? See counties reporting mosquito-borne illnesses According to the Florida Department of Health, as of Aug. 22, there have been 41 cases of locally acquired cases of dengue reported, along with 2 cases of West Nile Virus: Dengue: 41 cases 30 in Hillsborough County 7 in Miami-Dade County 1 in Palm Beach County 3 in Pinellas County West Nile: 2 1 in Alachua County 1 in Walton County Dengue in Florida: What symptoms should you watch for? "Dengue fever can be a painful, debilitating disease but is rarely fatal," the Florida Department of Health said. Symptoms appear three to 14 days after the bite of an infected mosquito and include: sudden onset of fever severe headache eye pain muscle and joint pain (giving the disease the nickname “breakbone fever”) bleeding Gastrointestinal symptoms like vomiting and diarrhea may also be present in some cases. "Dengue fever symptoms usually lasts four to seven days. The disease is often diagnosed incorrectly because the symptoms are similar to influenza and other viruses." Is dengue contagious? Dengue is spread by mosquitoes. Mosquitoes become infected with dengue after biting a person with the virus. Those infected mosquitoes then spread the virus to other people through bites. The Centers for Disease Control said a woman who is infected with the dengue virus during pregnancy could pass the virus to her fetus, causing death to the fetus, low birth weight or premature birth. What are symptoms of West Nile virus? "West Nile virus is a mosquito-borne flavivirus that causes mild to severe illness," the Florida Department of Health said. "Approximately 80% of people who are infected with WNV will not show any symptoms at all." Mild symptoms include: fever headache body aches nausea vomiting Sometimes swollen lymph glands or a skin rash on the chest, stomach and back. Symptoms can last from a few days to several weeks. About one in 150 people infected with West Nile will develop severe illness. Symptoms include: high fever headache neck stiffness stupor disorientation coma tremors convulsions muscle weakness vision loss numbness paralysis "These symptoms may last several weeks, and neurological effects may be permanent. Severe illness can also result in death," FDOH said. "Zika symptoms usually last for several days to a week," FDOH said. "People usually do not get sick enough to go to the hospital. For this reason, many people might not realize they have been infected." What are the best mosquito repellents to use? According to the Florida Department of Health, repellents with the following active ingredients provide the best protection: DEET Picaridin IR3535 Oil of lemon eucalyptus Para-menthane-diol 2-undecanone, a plant-derived ingredient 'Drain and cover' helps keep mosquitoes away The FDOH offered this advice to help keep mosquitoes away from your Florida home: Drain Drain water from garbage cans, old tires, pool covers, or any other containers where water collects. Empty and clean birdbaths and pet water bowls at least once or twice a week. Protect boats and vehicles from rain with tarps that don’t accumulate water. Maintain swimming pools in good condition and ensure they are appropriately chlorinated. Empty plastic swimming pools when not in use. Cover If you must be outside when mosquitoes are active, cover up. Apply mosquito repellent to exposed skin and clothing. Use mosquito netting to protect children younger than 2 months old. Just In Pakistan Opinion World Business Sports Tech Life & Style Health Today's Paper Magazines TNS Instep Money Matters YOU US Wednesday September 02, 2026 Today's Paper Prayer Timing Epaper Home Just In Pakistan Opinion World Business Sports Tech Life & Style Health Magazines Instep Money Matters YOU US TNS Risk factors for dengue transmission in existence Islamabad By Muhammad Qasim September 01, 2026 Relatives sit next to patients suffering from dengue fever at a hospital. — AFP/File Rawalpindi:A total of six individuals have so far tested positive for dengue infection from Rawalpindi district this year though the chances of incidence and spread of the infection is higher in the region because all the risk factors including rains, rising temperature, humidity and infected persons or travellers considered to be the most important for appearance of seasonal dengue fever have become very much in existence. Data collected by ‘The News’ on Monday has revealed that to date, a total of 3,450 individuals were screened for dengue fever of which 3,252 were taken as suspected cases of the disease however, only six were confirmed positive for the infection. Data also reveals that the infection has claimed no life in the district so far this year. It is important to mention that the teams of District Health Authority Rawalpindi have been finding the larvae of ‘aedes aegypti’, the vector that causes dengue fever, in abundance during indoor and outdoor surveillance in the district. The daily report prepared on dengue prevention and control by District Health Authority Rawalpindi on Monday has revealed that to date, larvae of ‘aedes’ mosquitoes have been found from a total of 106993 houses and 17,348 spots in the district. It is alarming that the teams of DHA have found larvae of ‘aedes’ mosquitoes from well over 32,000 houses and 4,300 spots in the district in the last two weeks. According to health experts, the situation regarding dengue transmission is well under control at the time yet the stage is set for a possible outbreak of the infection in the coming days or weeks as six individuals have already been tested positive for dengue fever in the district. It is important that confirmation of patients from a region increases the chances of spread of dengue fever. Studies reveal that the population of dengue fever vector fluctuates with temperature, rainfall and humidity. It is high in the rainy season and during the rainy season, the risk of virus transmission by the vector is greater. Epidemiological findings reveal that dengue infections generally occur during or after rain, as an outcome of rise in vector population. Preventing or reducing dengue virus transmission depends entirely on controlling the mosquito vectors or interruption of human-vector contact. Health experts say that individuals should take preventive measures religiously to avoid an outbreak of the infection. It is important that the effects of temperature, humidity and rainfall along with artificial flooding, the man-made watering of lawns, fields and water spillage, are the genuine factors besides movement of infected patients to cause fast spread of the disease. 时间

古巴拉斯图纳斯,重点关注儿童、老年人、孕妇、有潜在健康问题的个人以及与母婴护理计划相关的人员,这些人被视为高危人群。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue Type 4 Detected in Las Tunas: Vulnerable Groups Include Children, Pregnant Women, and the Elderly

News Brief
On September 2, 2026, health officials in Las Tunas, Cuba, confirmed the presence of dengue serotype 4, which is known for its greater clinical complexity. Children, the elderly, pregnant women, and individuals with underlying health conditions are identified as the most vulnerable groups. The confirmation has prompted a "sanitary offensive" by provincial authorities, including state inspections in workplaces and educational institutions to eliminate mosquito breeding grounds. The Pedro KourÃ Institute of Tropical Medicine in Havana is involved in virus typing and quality control. Authorities have urged the public to take symptoms seriously, emphasizing the need for prompt medical attention to prevent further complications. This alert is part of a broader context of dengue challenges in Cuba, with previous fumigation campaigns and ongoing concerns about mosquito activity and disease detection. The situation remains under active investigation and management, with no indication of resolution yet.
Original source text (English)
CubaHeadlines Dengue Type 4 Detected in Las Tunas: Vulnerable Groups Include Children, Pregnant Women, and the Elderly Wednesday, September 2, 2026 by Matthew Diaz Pregnant woman (Reference image) - Image by © Periódico 26 Health officials in Las Tunas have confirmed the presence of dengue serotype 4 in the region, with children, the elderly, pregnant women, and individuals with underlying health conditions identified as the most vulnerable to severe complications. On Wednesday, the state-run newspaper, PeriÃ3dico 26, reported that Dr. Yumara Acosta GarcÃa, the director of the Provincial Center for Hygiene, Epidemiology, and Microbiology, verified that dengue type 4 is currently prevalent in the area. Dr. Acosta emphasized that this serotype presents "greater clinical complexity" and mentioned that confirmed cases are sent to the Pedro KourÃ Institute of Tropical Medicine (IPK) in Havana for quality control and virus typing. Increased Alert and Preventive Measures Authorities have urged the public to take symptoms seriously. "If you experience joint pain, muscle pain, headaches, or fever, see a doctor to promptly identify cases and prevent further complications in this particular area," advised Acosta. The focus is particularly strong on children, the elderly, those with comorbid conditions, and pregnant women, as well as individuals linked to the Maternal and Child Care Program, all considered high-risk for potential complications. Sanitary Offensive and Inspections The confirmation of serotype 4 has prompted provincial authorities to launch a "sanitary offensive," conducting state inspections in workplaces and educational institutions. Workplaces are deemed "sentinels" due to the large number of people they gather. Inspections target the elimination of potential breeding grounds for the Aedes aegypti mosquito and ensure proper coverage of water storage containers. "Our focus from vector control is to prevent the spread of the vector and, consequently, the transmission of the disease from our workers to the broader community," explained the director. Background of Epidemiological Challenges Las Tunas previously faced significant epidemiological challenges. Last November, the province initiated a fumigation campaign in response to rising cases of fever, dengue, and chikungunya, prioritizing areas like Las Tunas, Puerto Padre, Majibacoa, and Jobabo, which had the highest incidence of suspected cases. This current alert is not isolated within the country. In June, the Ministry of Public Health warned of the risk of a new dengue epidemic, acknowledging that all four virus serotypes were circulating simultaneously in Cuba. Deputy Minister Carilda PeÃ±a noted that the dominance of any serotype could trigger localized outbreaks and, without proper measures, lead to an epidemic. More recently, the situation has also raised alarms in GuantÃ¡namo. In mid-August, officials acknowledged an increase in Aedes aegypti mosquitoes and challenges in detecting febrile cases. Serotypes 2, 3, and 4 were confirmed in that province, with several municipalities facing particularly concerning epidemiological situations. Understanding Dengue Type 4 and Its Impact in Cuba What is dengue serotype 4? Dengue serotype 4 is one of the four variations of the dengue virus, each capable of causing dengue fever. Infection with one serotype provides immunity against that serotype but not the others, making subsequent infections potentially more severe. Why are children and the elderly more at risk? Children and the elderly have weaker immune systems, making them more susceptible to severe complications from dengue infections, including dengue hemorrhagic fever and dengue shock syndrome. How is Cuba managing the dengue outbreak? Cuba is implementing sanitary measures including state inspections to eliminate mosquito breeding grounds, focusing on high-risk areas, and ensuring rapid identification and treatment of suspected cases to control the outbreak. Dengue cases rise in several Florida counties, CDC data shows - ABC7 WWSB

News Brief
The news report highlights an increase in dengue cases in several Florida counties, as per CDC data. Published on August 28, 2026, the report notes that Hillsborough County saw a rise from 20 to 36 cases, Miami-Dade from 49 to 67, and Broward from 11 to 12. Lee County moved from the CDC’s “1 to 4” range to five confirmed cases. The CDC acknowledges that the data is provisional and may be incomplete due to reporting delays. Florida health officials emphasize that many cases are imported from regions where dengue is endemic, such as the Caribbean, Central and South America, and Asia. Sarasota County monitors mosquito populations and tests for viruses, with response measures including increased trapping and investigation of breeding sites if mosquitoes test positive. The report also mentions that the CDC’s data is reported by the patient’s county of residence, not necessarily where the exposure occurred. The Florida Department of Health notes that dengue is a mosquito-borne disease related to West Nile and yellow fever, but it is rarely fatal. The report underscores the importance of monitoring and public precautions to mitigate the risk of local transmission.

(WWSB) - CDC data shows reported dengue cases have increased in several Florida counties. Hillsborough County rose from 20 reported cases to 36, according to the latest provisional county-level data. Miami-Dade County increased from 49 cases to 67. Broward County increased from 11 to 12 cases. Lee County moved from the CDC’s previous “1 to 4” reporting category to five cases. Because the earlier total was reported as a range, the exact size of the increase cannot be determined from the data provided. Pinellas County remained in the CDC’s “1 to 4” category. That does not necessarily mean the number of cases is unchanged, since the CDC does not provide an exact total for counties in that range. Dengue Fever reported in Tampa, Hillsborough County Palm Beach County remained at seven reported cases. Sarasota and Manatee counties were not listed in the latest county-level CDC table or in the data reviewed for WWSB’s Aug. 26 report. The CDC said its surveillance data is reported by a patient’s county of residence, not necessarily where the person was exposed to the virus. Dengue is a mosquito-borne disease related to the viruses that cause West Nile and yellow fever, according to the Florida Department of Health. The department says it can be painful and debilitating, though it is rarely fatal. Florida health officials say cases are reported each year among people who traveled to places where dengue is present. Those imported cases commonly involve travel to the Caribbean, Central and South America, and Asia. Cyclosporiasis cases rise in Manatee, Sarasota counties Florida’s proximity to areas with dengue, including Puerto Rico, and frequent international travel by residents make it possible for dengue to be reintroduced, the Florida Department of Health said. Sarasota County Mosquito Management also warns that someone who is infected while traveling can return home and be bitten by a local mosquito, which could then pass the disease to another person. Sarasota County Mosquito Management said it regularly monitors mosquito populations for potential disease. The county uses specialized traps to collect mosquitoes, then tests collected mosquitoes for viruses in an on-site molecular laboratory. The county said mosquito season typically peaks from May through October, when warmer temperatures and rain create conditions favorable for mosquitoes. If mosquitoes test positive for a virus, the county said it can increase trapping, investigate possible breeding sites and take other response steps. If a human case is reported, Sarasota County said those efforts continue while the Florida Department of Health gathers information about recent travel that could help determine where the infection was acquired. Sarasota County distinguishes between a mosquito-borne illness advisory and an alert. An advisory means the public should take precautions to reduce mosquito bites because of factors that can indicate local disease activity, such as positive sentinel animals or a small number of presumptive or positive human cases, the county said. An alert indicates heightened disease activity, a significant increase in mosquitoes capable of carrying disease or an increase in locally transmitted human cases, according to the county. CDC data shows a total of 1,575 reported human dengue cases so far in 2026. CDC said its data is provisional and subject to change. Delays in reporting mean state, territorial and local health departments may have more current totals. The data is provisional and subject to change. The CDC also warns that recently reported case numbers may be incomplete because of delays in reporting and data processing. Copyright 2026 WWSB. All rights reserved. This case was not listed in the Florida Department of Health's weekly report as of August 22, 2026. The Florida Department of Health reported more than 40 locally-acquired cases and over 100 travel-associated cases in 2026. Dengue is spread by Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus mosquitoes, which are found in Florida. Symptoms include sudden fever, severe headache, eye pain, muscle and joint pain, and can be fatal if untreated. Health officials are expanding surveillance and prevention efforts, including targeted mosquito spraying. Orange County Mosquito Control emphasized that the virus is spread only by infected mosquitoes and that all chemicals used are approved and safe for humans and animals. The case was confirmed on September 2, 2026, and the response includes public education on preventing mosquito bites.

Dengue is spread through the bite of an infected mosquito; symptoms are similar to the flu. Health officials said it's working with the area mosquito control agency to expand surveillance and prevention efforts. ORANGE COUNTY, Fla. - Florida health officials have confirmed a locally acquired case of dengue fever in Orange County. The state health department said Wednesday that it's working with Orange County Mosquito Control to expand surveillance and prevention efforts in the affected areas. Dengue cases in Florida By the numbers: More than 40 locally-acquired cases of dengue fever have been confirmed in Florida so far in 2026, along with more than 100 cases of travel-associated cases of dengue, according to the Florida Department of Health. 41: Number of dengue cases acquired in Florida in 2026, through Aug. 22. - Hillsborough: 30 - Miami-Dade: 7 - Palm Beach: 1 - Pinellas: 3 On Sept. 2, the Florida Department of Health reported one confirmed case of dengue in Orange County, which was not yet listed in the Florida Department of Health's weekly report. 109: Number of travel-associated dengue cases reported. - Bay: 1 - Brevard: 2 - Broward: 12 - Duval: 1 - Hillsborough: 6 - Lake: 1 - Lee: 5 - Manatee: 1 - Miami-Dade: 60 - Monroe: 1 - Orange: 2 - Osceola: 1 - Palm Beach: 6 - Pinellas: 1 - Polk: 2 - Sarasota: 1 - Seminole: 1 - St. Johns: 1 - St. Lucie: 3 - Walton: 1 For those cases connected to international travel, the exposure was connected to the following countries: Brazil, Colombia, Cuba, Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Guatemala, Guyana, India, Indonesia, Mexico, Nicaragua, Peru, and Puerto Rico. What is dengue? Dengue is a virus spread through bites from two species of mosquitoes, Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus, according to Florida health officials. Both are found in Florida. When someone is infected, it can lead to dengue fever, a sometimes painful, debilitating disease. While rare, if symptoms are left untreated, it can be deadly. Dengue fever symptoms Symptoms typically appear 3-14 days after being bitten by an infected mosquito, and usually last 4-7 days. Symptoms are similar to those of influenza and other viruses: Sudden fever Severe headache Eye pain Muscle and joint pain Bleeding Vomiting and diarrhea Can it be deadly? Dengue hemorrhagic fever, a more severe form of dengue, is rare, but can be deadly if not recognized and/or treated with supportive care, according to the Florida Department of Health's website. Treatment There is no treatment for dengue fever or dengue hemorrhagic fever. However, people should manage symptoms and complications. Tips for avoiding mosquito bites There are a number of mosquito-borne illnesses found in Florida, including West Nile, Eastern equine encephalitis, St. Louis encephalitis, as well as travel-related illnesses, like chikungunya fever, dengue fever, malaria, yellow fever, and Rift Valley fever. Health officials are urging residents to take steps to prevent mosquito bites: Use insect repellent on exposed skin or clothing Remove standing water to stop mosquitoes from breeding Keep doors, exterior windows closed Orange County Mosquito Control focused on mosquito spraying What they're saying: Orange County Mosquito Control said Wednesday that it would focus its spraying on the type of mosquito known to carry dengue. "This virus is spread only by mosquitoes," said Steve Harrison, Manager of Orange County Mosquito Control, in a statement. "A person can contract dengue only after being bitten by a mosquito carrying the virus. The mosquito becomes infected by biting a person who has the virus." Orange County Mosquito Control regularly sprays for mosquitos around the county, and said all chemicals are approved by the U.S. EPA and are considered to be safe for humans and animals. The Source: This story was written with information from the Florida Department of Health's Orange County office, the Florida Department of Health website on dengue fever, the Florida Arbovirus Surveillance weekly report (ending Aug. 22), and a statement from Orange County Mosquito Control issued on Sept. 2, the same day that the locally-acquired case in Orange County was confirmed. Yemen : Residents Report Dengue Fever Outbreak in Al-Matoun District, Al-Jawf - Yemen Online

News Brief
A dengue fever outbreak has been reported in Al-Matoun district, Al-Jawf governorate, Yemen, according to local residents and a health official. The outbreak was confirmed on August 30, 2026, with multiple deaths reported in recent days. Health workers have warned that the lack of timely intervention and medical supplies is exacerbating the situation, leaving families vulnerable. Hospitals and clinics in the area are struggling to manage the crisis, as preventive measures and awareness campaigns are absent. Residents have accused the Houthi authorities of failing to respond adequately to the outbreak, despite repeated appeals for assistance. This adds to Yemen’s ongoing public health challenges, which have been worsened by years of conflict and weakened infrastructure.

Nearly half of Tacloban barangays log dengue cases - Inquirer.net

News Brief
A surge in dengue cases has been reported in Tacloban City, Leyte, Philippines, as of August 26, 2026. Nearly half of the city’s 138 barangays have logged confirmed cases, with the total reaching 323. The Tacloban City Hospital treated 180 dengue patients from the start of the year through the previous week, with only six cases originating outside the city. Health officials have intensified mosquito-control efforts and urged residents to eliminate breeding sites, particularly in densely populated areas. The City Health Office warned that frequent rains could create favorable conditions for Aedes mosquitoes, which transmit the dengue virus, potentially leading to more cases in the coming months. The situation is being actively managed, with community involvement emphasized to prevent further spread.

More Than 40 Locally Acquired Dengue Cases Reported in Florida; FMCA and Mosquito Control Professionals to Help Prevent Spread - StreetInsider
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 31 日,佛罗里达州报告了 40 多例本地获得的登革热病例,其中希尔斯伯勒县 30 例、迈阿密戴德县 7 例、皮尼拉斯县 3 例、棕榈滩县 1 例。佛罗里达州蚊虫控制协会 (FMCA) 和蚊虫控制专业人员正在努力防止蚊媒疾病的传播。佛罗里达州希尔斯伯勒县卫生部发布了蚊媒疾病警报,强调了全年蚊虫 监测 和控制的重要性。登革热在人类中不会传染,但会引起严重的症状,包括肌肉和关节疼痛、发烧和皮疹。该疾病由埃及伊蚊传播,叮咬后 15 天内出现症状。FMCA 正在与卫生官员和地方部门合作,提供蚊子安全教育并治疗繁殖区。该州有 67 个蚊虫控制项目,包括 15 个独立区,这些项目共同努力共享资源并响应当地的 治疗 请求。暴发 强调需要提高公众意识并采取积极措施来减少蚊子繁殖的机会,例如排出积水和使用驱虫剂。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

More Than 40 Locally Acquired Dengue Cases Reported in Florida; FMCA and Mosquito Control Professionals to Help Prevent Spread - StreetInsider

News Brief
On August 31, 2026, more than 40 locally acquired dengue cases were reported in Florida, with 30 in Hillsborough County, 7 in Miami-Dade County, 3 in Pinellas County, and 1 in Palm Beach County. The Florida Mosquito Control Association (FMCA) and mosquito control professionals are working to prevent the spread of mosquito-borne illnesses. The Florida Department of Health in Hillsborough County issued a mosquito-borne illness alert, highlighting the importance of year-round mosquito surveillance and control. Dengue is not contagious among humans but can cause severe symptoms, including muscle and joint pain, fever, and a rash. The disease is transmitted by Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, and symptoms appear within 15 days of a bite. The FMCA is collaborating with health officials and local departments to provide mosquito safety education and treat breeding areas. The state has 67 mosquito control programs, including 15 independent districts, that work together to share resources and respond to local treatment requests. The outbreak underscores the need for public awareness and proactive measures to reduce mosquito breeding opportunities, such as draining stagnant water and using insect repellants.

The confirmed cases, which prompted the Florida Department of Health in Hillsborough County to issue a mosquito-borne illness alert, underscore the importance of Florida's year-round mosquito surveillance, prevention and control efforts. The locally acquired cases reported in Florida this year include 30 in Hillsborough County, seven in Miami-Dade County, three in Pinellas County and one in Palm Beach County. Hillsborough, Miami-Dade and Pinellas counties are currently under mosquito-borne illness alerts, while Palm Beach County is under a mosquito-borne illness advisory. In Hillsborough County, 10 mosquito pools have also tested positive for dengue virus. "Dengue is not simply an uncomfortable illness—it can be debilitating and, in severe cases, life-threatening," said Peter Jiang, PhD, director of Hillsborough County Mosquito Management and the president of the Florida Mosquito Control Association, (FMCA). "It is sometimes called 'break-bone fever' because of the intense muscle, joint and bone pain it can cause. For Floridians, it is an important reminder that mosquito control is not just about preventing a nuisance; it is an essential public health service that helps protect our residents and communities from mosquito-borne disease." The number of human cases is expected to increase in the state's weekly report. Florida's summertime climate is perfect for mosquitoes, and an influx of visitors – especially in popular destinations like Tampa - enjoying a summer vacation raises the opportunities for diseases to spread. Florida is home to some of the nation's top mosquito control scientists and professionals. Coordinating with the Florida Department of Health surveillance efforts are focused on limiting the spread. "And while success is never final, every resident and visitor to our state should know that Florida remains safe and accessible," he said. "Scientists and highly trained mosquito control professionals work closely with health officials 24/7 to limit the spread of any diseases." The state Department of Health says that dengue can "present as a flu-like illness with severe muscle aches and joint pain, fever, and sometimes a rash." Symptoms appear within 15 days of being bitten by an infected Aedes aegypti mosquito. The disease is not contagious among humans. For more than 100 years, the FMCA has helped scientists and entomologists battle mosquitoes and educate the public about the multitude of dangerous diseases transmitted via a mosquito bite. Currently, Hillsborough County Mosquito Management continues to work closely with the Florida Department of Health and various county and city departments to provide boots on the ground mosquito safety education in areas of concern. Florida's mosquito control efforts focus on scientific discovery and inquiry, front-line deployment of personnel and technology, and a strong network of professionals sharing best practices and experiences. In addition to treating areas where mosquitoes are heavily concentrated, counties use sentinel animals to test for some mosquito-spread diseases. Aircraft and drones can be deployed to treat inaccessible areas. Every Floridian can help, especially during hot weather which speeds up the lifecycle of a mosquito. The American Mosquito Control Association has a summary of measures , calling them the three Ds of protection. Drain –Following a rain or storm, each household and property becomes a virtual resort for mosquitoes looking for a place to breed. Emptying any water in toys or planters or anywhere is a critical and easy step to slow or eliminate the opportunity for mosquitoes to lay eggs. Mosquitoes only need a teaspoon of water to breed, emptying even small accumulations of water outside can make a difference. Dress – Wearing light-colored, loose-fitting clothes are a deterrent to mosquitoes, long sleeves or pants provide even more protection. Defend – Use insect repellants that are registered by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, especially those with DEET. They pose little to no risk for humans but are powerful shields against mosquitoes. There are 67 mosquito control programs in the state, including 15 independent districts that partner with each other and local departments of health to share important news, resources and scientific studies. They also stand ready to respond to any Floridian's request for local mosquito treatment. For more information about the FMCA please click here. And for teachers and parents, the FMCA has resources to inform children about the science and background of mosquitoes and efforts to control them here. B routine priorityPublic-health news

More Than 40 Locally Acquired Dengue Cases Reported in Florida; FMCA and Mosquito Control Professionals to Help Prevent Spread

News Brief
In Florida, more than 40 confirmed cases of locally-acquired dengue fever have been reported, with the highest number in Hillsborough County (30 cases), followed by Miami-Dade (7), Pinellas (3), and Palm Beach (1). The Florida Mosquito Control Association (FMCA) and mosquito control professionals are collaborating to prevent the spread of the disease. The Florida Department of Health has issued mosquito-borne illness alerts in Hillsborough, Miami-Dade, and Pinellas counties, and advisories in Palm Beach County. The outbreak highlights the importance of mosquito surveillance and control efforts, as dengue is not contagious among humans but can cause severe symptoms. The state's mosquito control programs, including 15 independent districts, are working with local health departments to implement measures such as treating high-risk areas, using sentinel animals for testing, and deploying aircraft and drones. Public education campaigns are also underway, emphasizing the 'three Ds' of protection: drain, dress, and defend against mosquitoes. The outbreak underscores the need for continued vigilance and community involvement in preventing mosquito-borne illnesses.
Original source text (English)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着病例上升,美国向肯尼亚发出登革热旅行警报 - Hiiraan Online

US issues dengue travel alert for Kenya as cases rise - Hiiraan Online
新闻简报由于登革热病例上升,美国于2026年8月7日向肯尼亚发布旅行健康警报,敦促旅行者采取预防蚊虫叮咬的预防措施。该警报特别强调肯尼亚沿海地区、瓦吉尔县和加里萨县是值得关注的地区。加里萨卫生当局通过加里萨县转诊医院和国家公共卫生实验室的实验室检测确认了登革热感染,40 个样本中有 28 个检测呈阳性。暴发 已导致患者入院治疗,并需要进行为期两周的熏蒸以消灭伊蚊。美国大使馆建议咨询疾病预防控制中心的旅行健康建议,并使用经美国环保署注册的驱虫剂、穿着防护服并在空调房内睡觉。建议患者监测症状,优先休息,并在出现警告信号时立即寻求医疗救助。登革热是一种由伊蚊传播的病毒感染,伊蚊在死水中繁殖,可引起发烧、疼痛和恶心,并可能导致严重并发症。
查看新闻五要素
时间
随着 登革热 病例增加,美国于 2026 年 8 月 7 日星期五发布了旅行健康警报。加里萨县的熏蒸演习于2026年5月星期四开始,持续了两周。
地点与对象
该警报覆盖肯尼亚沿海地区、瓦吉尔县和加里萨县。受影响的人群包括加里萨县的居民,特别是在加里萨县转诊医院住院的居民。
事件
由于 登革热 病例增加,美国驻内罗毕大使馆发布了针对肯尼亚的旅行健康警报。卫生当局通过加里萨县转诊医院和国家公共卫生实验室的实验室检测确认了登革热感染。
规模、影响与风险
从加里萨的公共和私人卫生机构收集的 40 个样本中,有 28 个样本的 登革热 检测呈阳性。登革热是一种由蚊子传播的病毒,可导致发烧、疼痛、恶心和皮疹,并可能导致严重并发症。该疾病主要通过受感染的伊蚊叮咬传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
美国大使馆建议旅行者查阅疾病预防控制中心的旅行健康建议,并采取措施避免蚊虫叮咬。加里萨县卫生官员进行了为期两周的熏蒸活动以消灭蚊子。实验室检测证实了暴发,敦促患者监测症状并在必要时寻求医疗帮助。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

US issues dengue travel alert for Kenya as cases rise - Hiiraan Online

News Brief
The United States issued a travel health alert for Kenya on August 7, 2026, due to a rise in dengue fever cases, urging travellers to take precautions against mosquito bites. The alert specifically highlights Kenya's coastal region, Wajir, and Garissa counties as areas of concern. Health authorities in Garissa confirmed dengue infections through laboratory tests at the Garissa County Referral Hospital and the National Public Health Laboratory, with 28 out of 40 samples testing positive. The outbreak has led to hospitalisations, prompting a two-week fumigation exercise to eliminate Aedes mosquitoes. The US Embassy recommends consulting the CDC's travel health advisories and using EPA-registered insect repellent, wearing protective clothing, and sleeping in air-conditioned rooms. Patients are advised to monitor symptoms, prioritise rest, and seek immediate medical assistance if warning signs appear. Dengue is a viral infection transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes, which breed in stagnant water, and can cause fever, aches, and nausea, with potential for severe complications.
Original source text (English)
by PERPETUA ETYANG Friday August 7, 2026 Image generated with AI The United States has issued a travel health alert for Kenya following a rise in dengue fever cases, urging travellers to take extra precautions against mosquito bites. In a health alert issued by the US Embassy in Nairobi, the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) listed Kenya among countries reporting a higher number of dengue cases in its updated Travel Health Notice. The advisory identifies Kenya's coastal region, as well as Wajir and Garissa counties, as areas where travellers should exercise caution. "Dengue is a mosquito-borne virus common in tropical and subtropical climates that can lead to fever, aches and pains, nausea, and rashes," the embassy said. The embassy urged travellers to consult the CDC's travel health advisories before travelling to affected areas and to take measures to avoid mosquito bites. Recommended precautions include using an Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)-registered insect repellent, wearing long-sleeved shirts and long trousers when outdoors, and sleeping in air-conditioned rooms or rooms fitted with window screens. The alert comes as health authorities continue to monitor dengue infections in several parts of the country, with the disease primarily transmitted through the bite of infected Aedes mosquitoes. The US Embassy advised travellers seeking more information to consult the CDC's travel health guidance or contact the agency directly before visiting affected areas. Dengue is a viral infection that affects millions of people worldwide and is transmitted through the bite of infected mosquitoes. Once diagnosed, it is important to follow medical advice closely, monitor symptoms, and prioritise rest. Adequate hydration plays a crucial role in preventing complications, while nutritious meals help support the body's recovery process. Patients should remain alert to warning signs that may indicate worsening illness and seek immediate medical assistance when necessary. In some cases, hospitalisation may be recommended to ensure close monitoring and appropriate treatment during the recovery period. In May, Garissa county embarked on a massive fumigation exercise aimed at containing the spread of dengue fever following a recent outbreak that has seen several residents hospitalised at the Garissa County Referral Hospital. The two-week exercise, which officially kicked off on Thursday, will involve public health officials fumigating drainage areas, public institutions, schools, markets and other public spaces across Garissa township in an effort to eliminate mosquitoes responsible for spreading the disease. Health officials said samples taken from patients admitted at the Garissa County Referral Hospital confirmed infections of dengue fever, a viral disease transmitted by the Aedes mosquito, which commonly breeds in stagnant water. Speaking to the press after overseeing the launch of the fumigation exercise, Hassan Abdullahi, who was representing the Medical Officer of Health said laboratory tests conducted at the National Public Health Laboratory confirmed the outbreak after 28 out of 40 samples collected from both public and private health facilities tested positive for dengue fever

The disease is primarily transmitted through the bite of infected Aedes mosquitoes.
Response, status and uncertainty

Laboratory tests confirmed the outbreak, and patients are urged to monitor symptoms and seek medical assistance when necessary.

柔佛登革热病例激增近10,000例,死亡13例 - newswav.com

Dengue surge in Johor hits nearly 10,000 cases, 13 deaths - newswav.com
新闻简报Johor, Malaysia, has seen a significant surge in dengue cases, with nearly 10,000 reported as of epid week 34 in 2026, marking a 131.3% increase compared to the same period last year. The state health and environment committee chairman, Ling Tian Soon, attributed this rise to the dominant dengue strain, DENV-3, which causes more severe infections and symptoms. Additionally, 13 deaths were recorded up to epid week 33, a 62.5% increase from 2025. The outbreak is concentrated in densely populated areas, particularly Johor Baru and Kulai, which accounted for 91% of cases. To combat the spread, Johor approved RM116,000 for fogging operations and increased community cleaning efforts. The current hot and dry weather, with intermittent rainfall, has created favorable conditions for Aedes mosquito breeding, which is essential for dengue transmission. The situation remains under active investigation, with no further details on the effectiveness of these measures provided.
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至 2026 年第 34 周,本期报告与去年同期相比出现激增。上个月,令提到了主要热点。
地点与对象
马来西亚柔佛州,特别提到新山、古来、峇株巴辖、昔加末、哥打丁宜、麻坡和笨珍。人口稠密,尤其是新山和古来,占病例的91%。
事件
柔佛州登革热病例激增,截至 2026 年第 34 周,报告病例数为 9,954 例,而去年同期为 4,304 例。激增归因于登革热主要菌株DENV-3和当前的天气条件。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年疫情第 33 周,该州记录了 9,954 例登革热病例和 13 例死亡,与 2025 年同期相比,病例增加了 131.3%,死亡人数增加了 62.5%。DENV-3 毒株与更严重的感染和症状有关。
应对、状态与不确定性
Johor has approved RM116,000 for fogging operations and increased community cleaning activities. The surge is attributed to DENV-3 and weather conditions, but the exact impact of these measures is not specified.

Malaysia's #1 Content Aggregator HomeContact UsAbout UsCareersCreator Back Home/ Local/ Dengue surge in Johor hits nearly 10,000 cases, 13 deaths Dengue surge in Johor hits nearly 10,000 cases, 13 deaths LocalHealth & Fitness 2 Sep 2026 • 1:28 PM MYT Scoop.my News You Can Use, Investigative Reports, Sports, Videos, and Analysis KUALA LUMPUR — Johor has recorded a drastic surge in dengue cases, rising 131.3% for the current period compared to the same period last year. In numbers, the state charted 9,954 dengue cases as of epid week 34 this year compared to 4,304 cases for the same period last year, Bernama reported state health and environment committee chairman Ling Tian Soon saying. Ling said of the total, 4,520 cases or 45.4% were reported from outbreak localities while 5,434 cases or 54.6% were from non-outbreak localities. By location, Ling said Johor Baru recorded the highest number of cases in epid week 34 with 495 cases or 74.6% of the total cases, followed by Kulai (120 cases), Batu Pahat (19), Segamat (nine), Kota Tinggi (seven), Muar and Pontian with four cases each. Johor has also recorded a total of 13 deaths due to dengue up to epic week 33 this year, compared to eight deaths for the same period in 2025, an increase of 62.5%. Last month, Ling said Johor Baru and Kulai were also the main dengue hotspots, accounting for 91% of cases in the state because of their dense populations. He also said the surge in cases was due to the dominant dengue strain — DENV-3 — that is now in circulation. Ling told The Star that this strain causes more severe infections and symptoms. The current hot and dry weather conditions, with intermittent rainfall, is also conducive for the breeding of Aedes mosquitoes which require only a small about of stagnant water. Johor last month approved an allocation of RM116,000 to the Health Department to support fogging operations and also increased the number of community cleaning activities or ‘gotong-royong’. - Sept 2, 2026 The post Dengue surge in Johor hits nearly 10,000 cases, 13 deaths appeared first on Scoop. The state recorded 9,954 dengue cases and 13 deaths up to epid week 33 in 2026, representing a 131.3% increase in cases and a 62.5% increase in deaths compared to the same period in 2025. The strain DENV-3 is associated with more severe infections and symptoms.

预防登革热和寨卡病毒的结构蓝图 - Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center

A structural blueprint for protection against Dengue and Zika - Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center
新闻简报弗雷德·哈钦森癌症中心的研究人员已经确定了一种结构蓝图,可以预防登革热和寨卡病毒。该研究于 2026 年 8 月 31 日发表,强调了这些病毒之间的密切关系,并表明针对共享表面蛋白的抗体可能提供广泛的保护。这项由 疫苗 和传染病部门的 Pancera 实验室进行的研究表明,某些抗体可以桥接相邻的包膜蛋白,从而阻止病毒进入。虽然这些发现很有希望,但它们仍然是理论性的,需要进一步验证。随着气候变化扩大了传播这些病毒的蚊子的地理范围,开发有效的 疫苗 变得越来越紧迫。该研究没有报告具体病例数、死亡数或暴露数,而是重点关注可为未来 疫苗 设计提供信息的结构和免疫学机制。
查看新闻五要素
时间
随着气候变化扩大了携带这些病毒的蚊子的范围,开发有效的 疫苗 变得越来越紧迫。
地点与对象
这两种病毒之间的密切关系似乎是 疫苗 设计的一个优势。
事件
弗雷德·哈钦森癌症中心的研究人员确定了针对登革热和寨卡病毒的潜在保护的结构蓝图,其研究结果发表在一篇科学焦点文章中。
规模、影响与风险
该研究强调了单个 疫苗 抵御多种相关病毒的潜力,但证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露数量。
应对、状态与不确定性
这项研究正在进行中,结构研究揭示了抗体如何针对登革热和寨卡病毒之间的共同特征。这些发现是理论上的,需要进一步验证。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

A structural blueprint for protection against Dengue and Zika - Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center

News Brief
Researchers at the Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center have identified a structural blueprint that could lead to protection against Dengue and Zika viruses. Published on August 31, 2026, the study highlights the close relationship between these viruses and suggests that antibodies targeting shared surface proteins might offer broad protection. The research, conducted by the Pancera Lab in the Vaccine and Infectious Diseases Division, reveals that certain antibodies can bridge neighboring envelope proteins, preventing viral entry. While these findings are promising, they remain theoretical and require further validation. As climate change expands the geographic reach of mosquitoes that transmit these viruses, the development of effective vaccines is becoming increasingly urgent. The study does not report specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, focusing instead on the structural and immunological mechanisms that could inform future vaccine design.

InvolvedToggle menu Attend an Event Giving Clubs Volunteer Contact Philanthropy Home chevron Hutch News chevron Science Spotlight chevron Science Spotlight more/close A structural blueprint for protection against Dengue and Zika more/close From the Pancera Lab, Vaccine and Infectious Diseases Division August 31, 2026 • By T Vasanthakumar Dengue and Zika are closely related viruses that threaten millions of people worldwide each year. As climate change expands the reach of the mosquitoes that carry these viruses, the development of effective vaccines is becoming increasingly urgent. The close relationship between the two viruses might seem like an advantage for vaccine design. Vaccines work by teaching the immune system to produce neutralizing antibodies, molecules that recognize and bind to a virus and block it from infecting cells. In theory, antibodies that recognize features shared by closely related viruses could provide protection against several of them at once. But with Dengue and Zika, that similarity comes with a catch. Dengue virus exists as four distinct serotypes and infection with one serotype doesn’t necessarily provide lasting protection against the others. Even more concerning, antibodies produced during one infection can sometimes recognize another serotype without effectively neutralizing it. Instead of blocking infection, these antibodies can actually help the virus enter cells and make a subsequent infection more severe. Add the closely related Zika virus to the mix, and the challenge becomes even greater. A successful vaccine must generate broadly neutralizing antibodies, capable of recognizing and blocking multiple related viruses without enhancing infection. Fred Hutch researchers previously discovered one such antibody, F25.S02, isolated from a patient who had experienced multiple exposures to both Dengue and Zika. Understanding exactly how F25.S02 recognizes and blocks all four dengue serotypes as well as Zika could provide valuable clues to design an effective vaccine. Researchers in the Vaccine and Infectious Disease Division at Fred Hutch have now uncovered the structural basis for F25.S02’s remarkable breadth. Led by Nicholas Hurlburt, a staff scientist in the Pancera Lab, the team visualized precisely how F25.S02 binds to viral proteins using cryogenic electron microscopy and X-ray crystallography, two techniques that allow scientists to reveal the structures of biological molecules and their interactions with atomic-level detail. By determining exactly how this antibody interacts with both viruses, researchers have uncovered clues that could help guide the design of next-generation vaccines. The broadly neutralizing antibody F25.S02 (yellow) binds to envelope proteins on the surface of Zika virus (blue). Structural studies revealed that the antibody bridges neighboring envelope proteins, locking them together and preventing the rearrangements needed for viral entry. The inset shows this interaction in atomic detail. Image created by Thamiya Vasanthakumar “By observing the structure of the most broadly neutralizing antibody to all four [Dengue] serotypes and Zika, you can understand its mode of action at the molecular level” explained Hurlburt. The structures revealed that F25.S02 targets a vulnerable seam between proteins found at the surface of the virus. Dengue and Zika viruses are coated in envelope proteins that help them enter human cells. These proteins must dramatically rearrange their structures to bring the viral and host cellular membranes together so they can fuse. This fusion step allows the virus to release its genetic material into the host cell. F25.S02 interferes with this process by binding precisely where two neighboring envelope proteins meet. By bridging this interface, the antibody acts like a molecular lock, holding the proteins together and preventing the rearrangement required for the virus to infect the cell. Importantly, the features recognized by F25.S02 are highly conserved, meaning they remain similar across all four dengue serotypes and Zika virus. While other parts of these viruses vary, this shared vulnerability gives F25.S02 a common target and helps explain its unusually broad protection. For Hurlburt, identifying that weak spot raises the obvious next question: can we make a vaccine that elicits broadly neutralizing antibodies that target this interface? The structural maps determined in this study could provide a roadmap for doing just that. By revealing exactly which features F25.S02 recognizes and how they are arranged in three dimensions, the structures could guide researchers in designing vaccines that direct the immune system toward this shared viral vulnerability and teach it to produce protective broadly neutralizing antibodies. The spotlighted research was supported by the J. B. Pendleton Charitable Trust, Fred Hutch Vaccine and Infectious Disease Division Initiative grant, the Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center Electron Microscopy Shared Resource, the Howard Hughes Medical Institute, and the National Institutes of Health. Hurlburt NK, Lubow J, Goo L, and Pancera M. 2026. Structural basis for antibody cross-neutralization of Dengue and Zika viruses. Communications Biology. DOI: 10.1038/s42003-026-09805-6. Thamiya Vasanthakumar Science Spotlight writer Thamiya Vasanthakumar is a postdoctoral research fellow in the Campbell Lab at Fred Hutch. As a structural biologist, she uses cryogenic electron microscopy (cryoEM) to visualize the molecular structures of receptors found on the surface of immune cells. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

卫生部在加里萨和瓦吉尔加强登革热应对工作,病例已达 1,678 例 - Hiiraan Online

Ministry intensifies dengue response in Garissa, Wajir as cases hit 1,678 - Hiiraan Online
新闻简报肯尼亚卫生部加强了对加里萨县和瓦吉尔县登革热 暴发 的应对力度,截至 2026 年 8 月 26 日,该县共报告了 1,678 例登革热病例。加里萨县累计报告病例 1,583 例,瓦吉尔县有 95 例实验室确诊病例。还报告了五人疑似死亡,其中四人在加里萨镇,一人在哈加德拉。加里萨的达达布地区病例数最多,为 1,276 例,其次是加里萨镇(251 例)和法菲(56 例)。在瓦吉尔,瓦吉尔东部确诊病例占89.5%。该部已启动公共卫生应急行动中心,并正在与县政府和合作伙伴进行协调。卫生机构处于高度戒备状态,可疑样本正被送往国家参考实验室进行确认。该部还强调了持续的监测的重要性,以验证监测的中断。措施包括消除蚊子滋生地、防止白天叮咬以及鼓励早期医疗护理。暴发 促使该部加强病例管理和感染预防方案,特别是针对 6 个月至 15 岁的儿童和青少年,他们占瓦吉尔确诊病例的 48.4%。应对措施包括风险沟通和社区参与,以减轻病毒的传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
卫生部于 2026 年 8 月 28 日星期五在加里萨县和瓦吉尔县加强了登革热应对措施。最新的县更新截至 2026 年 8 月 26 日,此后没有报告新病例。
地点与对象
加里萨县和瓦吉尔县报告了 暴发,特别是加里萨镇、达达布县、法菲县、东瓦吉尔县、埃尔达斯县、西瓦吉尔县、北瓦吉尔县、南瓦吉尔县、塔尔巴吉县和霍罗夫哈拉尔县。瓦吉尔确诊病例中,6个月至15岁的儿童和青少年占48.4%。
事件
截至 2026 年 8 月 26 日,卫生部报告加里萨县和瓦吉尔县共报告了 1,678 例登革热病例,其中加里萨县累计报告病例 1,583 例,瓦吉尔县累计报告病例 95 例。加里萨县还报告了五人疑似死亡。
规模、影响与风险
加里萨县报告的病例数最多,为 1,276 例,其次是加里萨镇,有 251 例,法菲有 56 例。在瓦吉尔,95 例确诊病例中,瓦吉尔东占 85 例。该部强调需要持续的监测来验证监测的中断。
应对、状态与不确定性
该部已在县和县以下协调会议的支持下启动了公共卫生应急行动中心。受影响地区的卫生机构处于高度戒备状态,可疑登革热样本正被转交给国家参考实验室进行确认。该部还加强了病例管理和感染预防和控制措施。

Ministry intensifies dengue response in Garissa, Wajir as cases hit 1,678 - Hiiraan Online

News Brief
The Ministry of Health in Kenya has intensified its response to a dengue outbreak in Garissa and Wajir counties, where a total of 1,678 cases have been reported as of 26 August 2026. Garissa County accounted for 1,583 cumulative reported cases, while Wajir County had 95 laboratory-confirmed cases. Five suspected deaths were also reported, with four in Garissa Township and one in Hagadera. Garissa's Dadaab area had the highest number of cases at 1,276, followed by Garissa Township (251) and Fafi (56). In Wajir, Wajir East represented 89.5% of the confirmed cases. The Ministry has activated the Public Health Emergency Operations Centre and is coordinating with county governments and partners. Health facilities are on high alert, and suspected samples are being sent to the national reference laboratory for confirmation. The Ministry has also emphasized the importance of sustained surveillance to verify the interruption of transmission. Measures include eliminating mosquito breeding sites, preventing daytime bites, and encouraging early medical care. The outbreak has prompted the Ministry to strengthen case management and infection prevention protocols, particularly for children and adolescents aged six months to 15 years, who make up 48.4% of confirmed cases in Wajir. The response includes risk communication and community engagement to mitigate the spread of the virus.

The health ministry noted that as at 26 August 2026, Garissa County had recorded 1,583 cumulative reported dengue cases, while Wajir County had recorded 95 laboratory-confirmed cases bringing the total to 1,678 cases. advertisements Five suspected deaths have been also been reported in Garissa County; four in Garissa Township and one in Hagadera. Further, the Ministry has strengthened case management and Infection Prevention and Control measures in affected health facilities as well as referral of suspected dengue samples to the national reference laboratory for confirmation, alongside priority investigation and classification of severe cases and suspected deaths. Ad Other measures being implemented include risk communication and community engagement focused on eliminating mosquito breeding sites, preventing daytime mosquito bites and encouraging early healthcare seeking. Dengue is transmitted mainly by infected Aedes mosquitoes, which commonly bite during the day and breed in small collections of stagnant water. The Ministry has urged households, institutions and communities empty, scrub and tightly cover water-storage containers at least once each week, dispose of bottles, tins, tyres and other items that can collect water, and clear blocked drains, use mosquito repellent, long clothing and window or door screens, especially during the day, and protect children from mosquito bites. Members of the public have also been advised to seek care promptly for fever, severe headache, pain behind the eyes, muscle or joint pain, nausea, vomiting or rash, get urgent medical attention for severe abdominal pain, persistent vomiting, bleeding, breathing difficulty, extreme weakness, restlessness or rapid deterioration. Ad These as well as use paracetamol for fever as directed by a health professional and avoid aspirin or ibuprofen because they may increase bleeding risk. Although no new cases have been reported since the latest county updates, the Ministry has reiterated commitment in sustained surveillance before interruption of transmission can be verified. It will continue working with county governments, health facilities, community health teams and partners to maintain clinical readiness, laboratory confirmation, vector control and timely public information. Advertisement Updated: 7:10 PM EDT Sep 1, 2026 Editorial Standards i Bernie Montás Reporter Lee County Mosquito Control on alert after Florida records 149 dengue cases Updated: 7:10 PM EDT Sep 1, 2026 Editorial Standards i Bernie Montás Reporter LEE COUNTY, Fla. — One of amateur bird photographer Joe Abel's biggest obstacles isn't capturing the perfect shot during his outings — it's battling mosquitoes. “As I'm walking, you know, I get a bird lined up, and I got to smack myself,” Abel said. Advertisement He admitted that he often forgets to use mosquito repellent. “I carry spray in my car. Most of the time, I forget to put it on,” he said. This rainy season, the risk of mosquito-borne diseases like dengue has increased. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reports 149 cases of dengue in Florida. The disease, transmitted by mosquitoes, can cause fever, nausea, rashes and pain in the muscles, joints or bones. Jenifer McBride, with the Lee County Mosquito Control District, shared how the disease has personally affected someone she knows. “My best friend's father lives in Puerto Rico, and last summer he was actually hospitalized with dengue,” McBride said. Of the 149 cases in Florida, 110 were contracted while the individuals were traveling. The remaining 39 cases were locally transmitted by mosquitoes. Dengue is transmitted by the Aedes aegypti mosquito, or the backyard mosquito, as McBride calls it. This insect lays eggs in places containing standing water, including bird baths and plants like bromeliads. McBride said the first line of defense against dengue is to dump standing water out of places that contain it. She and her team are on high alert as the disease spreads. “We're always concerned, but it certainly brings a new heightened awareness of being extra concerned, of making sure we're protecting our citizens,” she said. Although Lee County has not reported any locally transmitted dengue cases, McBride emphasized the importance of collaboration among mosquito control districts across the state. “We're all aware that we need to pitch in and make sure that we're not getting anything that's endemic, that we're fighting it and making sure it doesn't become something that we see every day,” she said. 研究发现,随着世界变得更加干燥,霍乱、西尼罗河和登革热可能会肆虐 - 降温

As the world gets drier, cholera, West Nile, and dengue can thrive, study finds - The Cool Down
新闻简报发表在《生态学趋势》和《进化》上的一项研究表明,尽管人们普遍认为缺水会降低疾病风险,但干旱会增加霍乱、西尼罗河病毒和登革热的传播。该研究分析了近 100 项涉及人类和其他动物的研究,揭示了一个“干旱-疾病悖论”,即干燥条件可以为某些病原体的繁殖创造机会。在索马里,2017 年的干旱与近 80,000 例霍乱病例同时发生,而加利福尼亚州 2000 年代的干旱与数以千计的谷热病例有关,而 2014 年的超级干旱则导致西尼罗河病毒病例数第二高。在巴西,干旱后的几个月内,登革热的危险可能会增加。该研究强调需要更好地跟踪水的变化和疾病风险,表明干旱规划应包括疾病预防和节水。科学家还指出,水管理决策可能会无意中创造有利于病原体的条件,这凸显了将健康考虑纳入干旱策略的重要性。

事件
发表在《生态学趋势》和《进化》上的一项研究表明,尽管人们普遍认为缺水会降低疾病风险,但干旱会增加霍乱、西尼罗河病毒和登革热的传播。该研究分析了近 100 项涉及人类和其他动物的研究,并描述了“干旱-疾病悖论”。
规模、影响与风险
研究发现,干旱有时可能与血吸虫病和疟疾的病例减少同时出现,但与霍乱、西尼罗河病毒和登革热的病例增加同时出现。在索马里,2017年的干旱与近80,000例霍乱病例同时发生。在加利福尼亚州,2000年代的干旱与数千例额外的球孢子菌病病例有关,而2014年的超级干旱则与第二高的西尼罗河病毒病例数相关。在巴西,干旱过后的几个月内,登革热的危险性可能会增加。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究强调需要更好地跟踪水的变化和疾病风险。改进的卫星监测可以帮助研究人员和官员预测疾病风险。研究人员还强调,水管理决策可以创造有利于病原体、寄生虫或携带它们的生物体的条件。干旱规划可能需要包括疾病预防和节水。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As the world gets drier, cholera, West Nile, and dengue can thrive, study finds - The Cool Down

News Brief
A study published in Trends in Ecology and Evolution suggests that drought can increase the spread of cholera, West Nile virus, and dengue, despite the common belief that water scarcity reduces disease risk. The research analyzed nearly 100 studies involving humans and other animals, revealing a 'drought-disease paradox' where dry conditions can create opportunities for certain pathogens to thrive. In Somalia, the 2017 drought coincided with nearly 80,000 cholera cases, while California's 2000s drought was linked to thousands of additional valley fever cases and the 2014 superdrought to the second-highest tally of West Nile virus cases. In Brazil, dengue danger can increase in the months following drought. The study emphasizes the need for better tracking of water shifts and disease risks, suggesting that drought planning should include disease prevention alongside water conservation. Scientists also note that water management decisions can inadvertently create conditions favorable to pathogens, highlighting the importance of integrating health considerations into drought strategies.
Original source text (English)

Tech As the world gets drier, cholera, West Nile, and dengue can thrive, study finds "We think a water-borne disease should thrive only when there's plenty of water." by Leigh CookAugust 30, 2026 share Facebook Twitter Link Copied! Photo Credit: iStock Drought is often framed as a water shortage problem, but scientists say it can also become a disease problem — and in some cases, a serious one. New research suggests that as landscapes dry out, infections such as cholera, West Nile virus, and dengue may be more likely to spread. Here's what to know According to Grist, a paper in Trends in Ecology and Evolution analyzed nearly 100 studies involving humans and other animals and described a pattern researchers call the "drought-disease paradox." Dry conditions can reduce some disease threats as water sources disappear, but those same conditions can also create opportunities for other pathogens to thrive. The review examined illnesses linked to mosquitoes, contaminated water, and shifting aquatic systems. It found that drought can sometimes coincide with lower cases of schistosomiasis and malaria, even as cholera, West Nile virus, and dengue move in the opposite direction. Tara Stewart Merrill, an aquatic disease ecologist at the Cary Institute of Ecosystem Studies and co-author of the paper, said the finding may sound surprising at first. TCD Picks » Quince Spotlight 💡These best-sellers from Quince deliver affordable, sustainable luxury for all This popular cashmere sweater just got an upgrade Starting at $50 Sleep serenely on these luxury sheets at 50% off retail Starting at $99 You'll love the pockets on this European linen dress Starting at $60 This cotton blazer's price is even smarter than its look Starting at $80 "It's so counterintuitive," she said, per Grist. "We think a water-borne disease should thrive only when there's plenty of water, but in some cases they thrive when there's less water." The paper is also notable because infectious disease research has historically paid more attention to heat than to drought, even as increasingly dry conditions affect many parts of the world. More background Warming air can draw additional moisture from the ground, worsening drought in many places. Citing a United Nations report, Grist said the world had become 77% drier in three decades. The researchers also highlighted outbreaks and case trends that have tracked with drought. Somalia's 2017 drought coincided with nearly 80,000 cholera cases. In California, the 2000s drought coincided with thousands of additional cases of valley fever, and the state's 2014 superdrought was associated with its second-highest tally of West Nile virus cases. In Brazil, Grist reported findings that dengue danger can increase in the months following drought. Drought can also shift when and where mosquito-borne, food-borne, and dust-borne illnesses appear. Georgia Titcomb, a wildlife disease ecologist and assistant professor at Colorado State University, added that when water becomes scarcer or more unevenly distributed, animals may change their movements and cluster at the remaining sources, creating new hotspots for transmission, Grist reported. What can be done? Scientists said better tracking will be a major part of the solution. Improved satellite monitoring could help researchers and officials track shifts in water across landscapes and better anticipate where disease risks may rise. The study also suggested that water management decisions matter. Building dams, expanding irrigation, or otherwise changing how water moves can sometimes create conditions that help pathogens, parasites, or the organisms that carry them. Drought planning may need to include disease prevention alongside water conservation. That can mean paying closer attention to changing mosquito activity, water quality concerns, and the health effects of dust after wet periods are followed by dry ones — a pattern researchers call "weather whiplash." Drought does not always reduce infection risk. In some places, it can instead shift that risk. As Samantha Sambado, a biologist and postdoctoral researcher at Stanford University who was not involved in the study, put it, according to Grist, "Water touches almost every aspect of life. How do we manage this resource, knowing the future will be warmer?" Get TCD's free newsletters for easy tips, smart advice, and a chance to earn $5,000 toward home upgrades. Global, with specific examples in Somalia, California, and Brazil.
Event
A study published in Trends in Ecology and Evolution suggests that drought can increase the spread of cholera, West Nile virus, and dengue, despite the common belief that water scarcity reduces disease risk. The study analyzed nearly 100 studies involving humans and other animals and described the 'drought-disease paradox.'.
Scale, impact and risk
The study found that drought can sometimes coincide with lower cases of schistosomiasis and malaria, but higher cases of cholera, West Nile virus, and dengue. In Somalia, the 2017 drought coincided with nearly 80,000 cholera cases. In California, the 2000s drought was linked to thousands of additional valley fever cases, and the 2014 superdrought was associated with the second-highest tally of West Nile virus cases. In Brazil, dengue danger can increase in the months following drought.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study highlights the need for better tracking of water shifts and disease risks. Improved satellite monitoring could help researchers and officials anticipate disease risks. The researchers also emphasized that water management decisions can create conditions that help pathogens, parasites, or organisms that carry them. Drought planning may need to include disease prevention alongside water conservation.
原文 / Source

The worst year on record for dengue in Bangladesh was 2023, with 321,179 hospitalizations and 1,705 deaths. The health directorate is monitoring the situation, and the information is based on official data.

According to the DGHS, as many as 88 people have died of dengue so far this year. The photo was taken from Mugda Medical College & Hospital in the capital on Friday. -Mostafizur Rahman, AA Bangladesh has logged 342 new dengue hospitalizations in the past 24 hours till 8am on Friday, taking the tally of admitted patients to 2,268. No deaths have been reported during the period, according to the Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) bulletin issued yesterday. The DGHS said 88 people have died of dengue so far this year, including 34 in August alone. Dhaka North City Corporation hospitals admitted 72 patients, while 43 were admitted under Dhaka South. By division, Barishal recorded 63 admissions, Chattogram 93, Mymensingh 48 and Rangpur 23. Meanwhile, 345 patients were discharged in the same 24-hour window. The DGHS said 32,590 people have been hospitalised with the mosquito-borne disease since Jan 1 this year, of whom 30,422 have recovered. The health directorate has tracked dengue cases since 2000. The worst year on record was 2023, when 321,179 people were hospitalised and 1,705 died, the highest annual toll to date. Last year, dengue killed more than 400 people, with around 102,000 cases recorded. Dengue (break-bone fever) is a viral infection that is spread from mosquitoes to people. It is more common in tropical and subtropical than in temperate climates. Most people who get dengue do not have symptoms. For those who do, the most common symptoms are high fever, headache, body aches, nausea and rash. Most get better in 1-2 weeks. Some develop severe dengue and need care in a hospital. In severe cases, dengue can be fatal. You can lower your risk of dengue by avoiding mosquito bites, especially during the day. Dengue is treated through pain management as there is no specific treatment currently.
