B routine priorityPublic-health news

Dengue-related kidney injury: Symptoms, risks, and warning signs - theweek.in

News Brief
The article from theweek.in, published on August 29, 2026, discusses dengue-related acute kidney injury (AKI), a serious but often overlooked complication of dengue infection. It highlights that AKI can range from a temporary decline in kidney function to severe renal failure requiring dialysis. The risk is higher in patients with severe dengue, shock, or severe bleeding, as well as those with pre-existing conditions like diabetes or hypertension. The article stresses the importance of early detection, monitoring urine output, and seeking immediate medical attention when symptoms worsen. It also notes that while many symptoms of AKI may overlap with those of dengue, medical evaluation and laboratory tests are crucial for accurate diagnosis. The piece does not report any specific outbreak or event but provides general health information on the topic.
Original source text (English)
SECTIONS journalism with a human touch Login My Profile Signed In as Profile Change Password Sign out Home Magazine Quick Read India World Sports Business Entertainment Reviews Columns Leisure More Hamburger Web Stories Videos Sci/Tech Health Defence Middle East Maritime Tourism Education Jobs & Career Search Subscribe Specials Middle East Defence Health Maritime × Search results for journalism with a human touch × News India World Sports Business Tourism Sci/Tech Entertainment Jobs & Career Education Magazine The Week Health Opinion Specials Health Defence Middle East Maritime Reviews Movies Books Gadgets Games Multimedia Videos Web Stories Quick Read Leisure Society Lifestyle Services About Us Privacy Policy Terms of Use Contact Us SIGN IN SUBSCRIBE Follow Us News Health Dengue-related kidney injury: Symptoms, risks, and warning signs Dengue can lead to acute kidney injury (AKI), a serious complication often overlooked, especially during monsoon seasons Dr Vikram Kalra Published: August 29, 2026 12:33 PM IST 4 minute Read Share × Email Facebook Twitter WhatsApp LinkedIn Telegram Reddit 0 Comments Link Copied Highlights Dengue can cause acute kidney injury (AKI), a serious but often overlooked complication, especially in severe cases The risk is highest in patients with severe dengue, shock, or dehydration, as these conditions reduce blood flow to the kidneys Early detection is crucial; monitor for warning signs like reduced urine output and seek immediate medical help if symptoms worsen Representation Monsoon season sees dengue emerge as one of the leading diseases carried by mosquitoes. The majority of individuals who are affected recover through proper treatment and management of the disease condition. Nonetheless, dengue can be severe in some individuals and affect vital organs such as the kidneys. Dengue-related acute kidney injury (AKI) is a key issue, though it is often overlooked. It can vary from a temporary decline in renal function to severely debilitating renal failure necessitating constant monitoring or, in exceptional cases, dialysis. The risk factor is generally higher in patients suffering from severe cases of dengue, often resulting in complications such as bleeding, shock, and severe dehydration. How can dengue affect the kidneys? Kidney injury in dengue can be due to various factors. Severe dehydration from fever, vomiting, and reduced fluid intake can decrease blood flow to the kidneys. In severe cases of dengue, plasma leakage can decrease effective circulating blood volume and cause hypotension, leading to inadequate blood supply to the kidneys. Inflammation due to dengue infection may also lead to renal damage. Moreover, complications like muscular injury, bleeding, shock, and electrolyte imbalances can put more pressure on the kidneys. Who is at greater risk? It is not true that every person suffering from dengue is going to develop any sort of kidney damage. However, the risk is higher for those who are suffering from serious dengue, shock, or severe bleeding. People with pre-existing impairment, diabetes, hypertension and other chronic conditions can also be monitored very carefully. What are the warning signs? The problem with acute kidney injury is that it can sometimes manifest with very few symptoms. As a result, a patient may not realise that his kidney function is declining until the damage becomes more serious, which makes it more difficult to treat the condition. The symptoms of acute kidney injury include lack of urine production, dark urine, swelling around the eyes, tiredness, feeling sick and vomiting, difficulty in breathing, confusion, and high blood pressure. It is important to point out that many of these symptoms may occur when a patient has dengue. This is why medical evaluation and laboratory examination are crucial when the patient’s condition is worsening. Another important thing to bear in mind is that just because the fever starts to go down, it doesn’t mean that the patient is recovering from his illness. In fact, critical moments in dengue are known to take place when the fever begins to decline. Therefore, patients should continue looking for any warnings even when their temperature becomes normal. How is kidney injury diagnosed? To evaluate the functioning of the kidneys, doctors can analyse urine production and conduct blood tests called serum creatinine and blood urea tests, which help to determine changes in the kidney filtering process, and electrolytes are studied to identify disruptions in sodium and potassium levels. The doctors might check the blood counts as well as the liver function based on the patient’s condition level and other factors. How is it managed? The treatment strategy will depend on the severity of dengue, hydration level, blood pressure, kidney function, and other complications. Fluid balance is important. Fluid administration must be adequate since both deficiency and excess can be detrimental. On one hand, too much fluid in a dehydrated patient may aggravate kidney injury, whereas too much fluid given to a patient suffering from kidney impairment may cause both fluid overload and respiratory distress. Severe cases of kidney injury must receive supportive care in the hospital, and for certain selected cases, dialysis may be necessary. Prevention starts with early recognition: The optimal method to minimise the chances of severe kidney complications is by detecting the warning signs at an early stage. A person who is infected with dengue should keep track of how much water is taken in and how much urine is produced, as well as listen to what the doctor has to say in terms of fluid intake, medications, and tests. Reduced urine output, continuing vomiting and bleeding, extreme fatigue and weakness, breathlessness, swelling, confusion, and increasing abdominal pain should be reasons for a person to seek immediate medical assistance. Starting off as a simple viral fever, dengue can evolve into more complicated forms in some cases. Monitoring urine output and other symptoms can help diagnose kidney disturbances at an early stage. The author is the principal director, Nephrology and Kidney Transplant at Aakash Healthcare Multi-Speciality Hospital, Dwarka. The opinions expressed in this article are those of the author and do not purport to reflect the opinions or views of THE WEEK. B routine priorityPublic-health news

As dengue fever spreads across Tampa Bay, health officials encourage safety measures - yahoo.com

News Brief
Dengue fever is spreading in the Tampa Bay area, prompting health officials to encourage safety measures. Pasco County confirmed a recent case, and local authorities are urging the public to take precautions to prevent the disease's spread. This follows reports of locally acquired cases in Hillsborough, Pasco, and Citrus counties this month. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, at least 149 confirmed dengue cases have been reported in Florida this year, the highest in the U.S. The Pasco County Mosquito Control District emphasizes that protection begins with keeping mosquitoes away from homes and individuals. They recommend measures such as draining standing water, using EPA-registered insect repellent, and wearing protective clothing. Health officials are currently investigating how and where the disease was transmitted, highlighting the ongoing nature of the situation and the need for community vigilance.
Original source text (English)
As dengue fever spreads across Tampa Bay, health officials encourage safety measures - Dengue fever cases are on the rise in the Tampa Bay area, with Pasco County confirming a recent case and health officials urging preventative measures. Dengue fever cases are increasing in the Tampa Bay area, and local health officials are asking people to take precautions to prevent the spread. Pasco County confirmed a case of dengue earlier this week. Health officials are investigating how and where the disease was transmitted. The Pasco County case comes after Hillsborough, Pasco and Citrus counties also reported locally acquired cases this month. The Pasco County Mosquito Control District says protection against dengue starts with keeping mosquitoes away from yourself and your home. They suggest the following measures to limit the chances of mosquito bites: - Dump and drain standing water around homes and businesses at least once a week. - Use EPA-registered insect repellent containing an active ingredient such as DEET, picaridin or IR3535. - Cover up with long sleeves, long pants, and socks when possible. - Close doors and keep windows properly screened. - Check your property for small containers and any items that can hold water. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, at least 149 confirmed cases of dengue have been reported in Florida this year — by far the most in the U.S. The latest CDC data, updated as of Aug. 26, lists at least 36 cases reported in Hillsborough County and one to four in Pinellas

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

棒球运动员赫克托·门多萨 (Héctor Mendoza) 的妹妹在青年岛的医院去世:“她到达时脱水,而且没有静脉注射液体。” - CiberCuba

Sister of baseball player Héctor Mendoza passes away in hospital on Isla de la Juventud: "She arrived dehydrated and there was no IV fluid." - CiberCuba
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 29 日,古巴棒球运动员埃克托·门多萨 (Héctor Mendoza) 的 27 岁妹妹玛丽亚·路易莎·门多萨 (María Luisa Mendoza) 因出血性登革热入院,在青年岛的医院去世。据专业媒体Swing Completo报道,她到达时已脱水,医院也缺乏治疗所需的血清。家人试图寻找所需的医疗用品但失败,导致她心脏骤停并死亡。这一事件凸显了古巴医院系统面临的严峻挑战,该系统自2026年2月以来一直处于紧张状态。到2026年4月,超过70%的基本药物缺货,医院缺乏静脉注射液等关键物资。健康危机与登革热疫情同时发生,截至 2025 年底,古巴报告了超过 81,900 例病例,至少 65 人死亡。青年岛当局没有就该病例发表任何官方声明,目前的情况被描述为古巴医院系统几十年来最糟糕的情况。报告还指出,2025 年和 2026 年古巴的其他病例也记录了医疗资源匮乏和疾病严重的情况。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该事件发生于 2026 年 8 月 29 日星期六,新闻发布于 2026 年 8 月 31 日星期一。健康危机自 2026 年 2 月以来一直持续,到 2026 年 4 月和 8 月情况进一步恶化。
地点与对象
该事件发生在青年岛,特别是新赫罗纳市的“Héroes del Baire”市立医院。受影响的人群包括 27 岁女性患者玛丽亚·路易莎·门多萨 (María Luisa Mendoza) 以及更广泛的古巴民众,特别是青年岛特别市的民众。
事件
古巴棒球运动员埃克托·门多萨的妹妹玛丽亚·路易莎·门多萨因出血性登革热在医院去世。医院缺乏治疗所需的血清,家人只好到医院外寻找,最终未能挽救她的生命。该事件是根据专业媒体Swing Completo的报道确认的。
规模、影响与风险
玛丽亚·路易莎·门多萨 (María Luisa Mendoza) 的死亡是一个个案,但它反映了古巴更广泛的健康危机,截至 2026 年 4 月,古巴 651 种基本药物中有 461 种缺货,医院缺乏静脉注射液等关键物资。目前的情况被描述为古巴医院系统几十年来最糟糕的时刻,重症登革热 疫情仍在持续。由于缺乏医疗资源和疾病的严重性,该风险被强调为严重。
应对、状态与不确定性
青年岛的卫生和政府当局没有就此案发表任何官方声明。健康危机仍在持续,短期内看不到解决方案,古巴的医院系统承受着巨大压力。该报告基于一位接受新闻媒体采访的消息人士的说法,这种情况被描述为 2025 年和 2026 年古巴的记录模式。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Sister of baseball player Héctor Mendoza passes away in hospital on Isla de la Juventud: "She arrived dehydrated and there was no IV fluid." - CiberCuba

News Brief
María Luisa Mendoza, the 27-year-old sister of Cuban baseball player Héctor Mendoza, died in the hospital in Isla de la Juventud on August 29, 2026, after being admitted with hemorrhagic dengue. According to the report from specialized media Swing Completo, she arrived dehydrated and the hospital lacked the necessary serum for treatment. The family attempted to find the required medical supplies but failed, leading to her cardiac arrest and death. This incident highlights the severe challenges faced by the Cuban hospital system, which has been under strain since February 2026. By April 2026, over 70% of essential medications were out of stock, and hospitals were lacking critical supplies like intravenous solutions. The health crisis coincides with a dengue epidemic, with Cuba reporting over 81,900 cases and at least 65 deaths by the end of 2025. The authorities in Isla de la Juventud did not issue any official statement on the case, and the situation is described as the worst for the Cuban hospital system in decades. The report also notes that the lack of medical resources and the severity of the disease have been documented in other cases in Cuba during 2025 and 2026.
Original source text (English)
Homepage Latest News Cuba News Miami News Blackouts today Informal Exchange Rate Videos Sports International News Opinion CiberCuba Interviews United States Cuisine Florida Spain Culture Motor Readings Tourism History Nature Health Close the sidebar ESENFRIT Sister of baseball player Héctor Mendoza passes away in hospital in Isla de la Juventud: "She arrived dehydrated and there was no IV." Latest News By CiberCuba Editorial Team Monday, August 31, 2026 - 12:30 María Luisa Mendoza, sister of the Pinero pitcher Héctor MendozaPhoto © Facebook/Swing Completo and Cantalo TV María Luisa Mendoza, 27 years old and sister of the Cuban pitcher Héctor Mendoza, passed away on Saturday at the municipal hospital "Héroes del Baire" in Nueva Gerona, Isla de la Juventud, after being admitted with a case of hemorrhagic dengue at a facility that did not have the necessary serum to treat her, according to the specialized media Swing Completo. A source who spoke with that news outlet described the events harshly: "She arrived dehydrated and there was no IV fluid. The family went out to find the necessary medical supplies, and when they returned, she went into cardiac arrest and couldn’t make it." Intravenous hydration is the essential treatment for hemorrhagic dengue, the severe form of the disease, which can lead to bleeding, shock, and multiple organ failure within a few hours. The health and governmental authorities of the special municipality Isla de la Juventud did not issue any official statement regarding the case. The passing of María Luisa Mendoza occurred at the worst moment for the Cuban hospital system in decades. In February, the Minister of Public Health, José Ángel Portal Miranda, acknowledged that the system was "on the verge of collapse", with no short-term solutions in sight. By April, 461 out of 651 essential medications—over 70% of the basic list—were out of stock in state pharmacies, and many hospitals were lacking pain relievers, antibiotics, suturing materials, and even intravenous solutions. The situation described by the source—serious patient, lack of serum, family going to seek it in the informal market, death during the wait—has been repeated in other documented cases in Cuba during 2025 and 2026. The health crisis coincides with a relentless dengue epidemic. Cuba closed 2025 with over 81,900 reported cases of dengue and chikungunya and at least 65 associated deaths, according to the Pan American Health Organization. The Ministry of Public Health warned in June about a possible dengue epidemic. The following month, Villa Clara confirmed the simultaneous circulation of serotypes DENV-2 and DENV-4, a combination capable of worsening the patient's condition within hours. By the end of August, Sancti Spíritus reported a steady increase in cases. Héctor Mendoza is one of the most recognized pitchers from Isla de la Juventud in the last decade. He made his debut in the National Series in 2011 and was selected for the Cuban national team for the 2014 Central American Games, the 2015 Pan American Games, and the Premier12 that same year. He later pitched for the Yomiuri Giants in Japan and in June 2017 signed with the St. Louis Cardinals for the minor leagues in the U.S., an organization that released him in 2019. As of the closing of this note, neither the player nor any other family member had made public statements regarding the death of María Luisa Mendoza. Time
The event occurred on Saturday, August 29, 2026, with the news published on Monday, August 31, 2026. The health crisis has been ongoing since February 2026, with the situation worsening by April and August 2026.
Location and population
The incident took place in Isla de la Juventud, specifically at the municipal hospital "Héroes del Baire" in Nueva Gerona. The affected population includes the patient María Luisa Mendoza, a 27-year-old woman, and the broader Cuban population, particularly those in the special municipality of Isla de la Juventud.
Event
María Luisa Mendoza, the sister of Cuban baseball player Héctor Mendoza, died in the hospital due to hemorrhagic dengue. The hospital lacked the necessary serum for treatment, and the family had to seek it outside the facility, which ultimately failed to save her life. This event is confirmed based on the report from the specialized media Swing Completo.
Scale, impact and risk
The death of María Luisa Mendoza is a single case, but it reflects a broader health crisis in Cuba, where 461 out of 651 essential medications were out of stock by April 2026, and hospitals lacked critical supplies like intravenous solutions. The situation has been described as the worst moment for the Cuban hospital system in decades, with a severe dengue epidemic ongoing. The risk is highlighted as critical due to the lack of medical resources and the severity of the disease.
Response, status and uncertainty
Health and governmental authorities in Isla de la Juventud did not issue any official statement regarding the case. The health crisis is ongoing, with no short-term solutions in sight, and the Cuban hospital system is under significant strain. The report is based on a source who spoke with the news outlet, and the situation is described as a documented pattern in Cuba during 2025 and 2026.
